Title: Online change point detection under heavy-tailedness and contamination

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.09737v1

Published Time: Tue, 09 Jun 2026 02:45:44 GMT

Number of Pages: 101

Markdown Content:
# Online change point detection under heavy-tailedness and contamination 

Edwin Y. N. Tang 1, Yudong Chen 1, Mengchu Li 2, and Yi Yu 11Department of Statistics, University of Warwick 

> 2

School of Mathematics, University of Birmingham June 9, 2026 

Abstract 

We study an online version of the robust mean change point detection problem under a dynamic Huber contamination model with arbitrary contamination distribution and inlier distribution pos-sessing exponentially- or polynomially-decaying tails. This robustness framework is systematically studied for the first time in the change point literature. For univariate data, we characterise the detection delay by partitioning the parameter space into four regimes, in terms of the true change location, signal size and contamination level. Efficient detection procedures are accompanied by matching lower bounds, up to poly-logarithmic factors. For the multivariate setting, we devise an efficient robust mean testing procedure and apply this to the robust online change point problem. The theoretical analysis of the robust mean testing procedure is the first in dealing with both Hu-ber contamination and heavy-tailedness, and is thus of independent interest. Extensive numerical experiments are conducted to support our theoretical findings. 

# 1 Introduction 

Change point analysis has a long history (e.g. Page, 1954; Lorden, 1971) and has experienced a marked renaissance in recent years, particularly due to its applications in fields such as finance, cybersecurity, and manufacturing. Modern technology enables the real-time monitoring of evolving processes, including stock market fluctuations (e.g. Chen and Gupta, 1997), internet traffic (e.g. Peng et al., 2004) and industrial quality control (e.g. Wadinger et al., 2024). For these streaming data, the ability to detect change points quickly and respond promptly is more desirable than conducting retrospective analyses on the entire dataset (Chen et al., 2022). However, the presence of outliers or heavy-tailedness can complicate this online detection task. Traditional methods, such as cumulative sum techniques (e.g. Wang and Samworth, 2018; Wang et al., 2020; Yu et al., 2023), that rely on Gaussianity or sub-Gaussianity assumptions often fail to perform effectively in these scenarios. To address these challenges, we leverage ideas from robust statistics to design robust online change point algorithms. The rest of the paper is organised as follows. Section 1.1 introduces the problem setup, followed by a summary of our main results in Section 1.2. A review of related work is presented in Section 1.3. In Section 2, we investigate the univariate problem, providing lower bounds on detection delay and presenting an optimal procedure with its theoretical guarantees. We then devise and analyse an efficient robust multivariate mean testing algorithm in Section 3, and apply this to the multivariate change point problem in Section 4. Simulation studies that verify our theoretical results are presented in Section 5. All the proofs are deferred to the Appendices. 

1.1 Problem setup 

We consider the online mean change point detection problem under both Huber contamination and heavy-tailed noise, for both univariate and multivariate data sequences. The model setup is detailed below. 1

> arXiv:2606.09737v1 [math.ST] 8 Jun 2026

1.1.1 Contamination and heavy-tailedness 

We consider a variant of the classical Huber contamination model (Huber, 1965) tailored for streaming data, namely the dynamic Huber ε-contamination model defined in Definition 1. This model has been previously studied in the context of offline robust change point detection in Li and Yu (2021). 

Definition 1 (Dynamic Huber ε-contamination model with inlier class D). Let p ∈ N, ε ∈ [0 , 1/2) 

and D be a class of distributions on Rp. Let {Qi}i∈N be a sequence of distributions on Rp. We say that {Qi}i∈N is a dynamic Huber ε-contamination model with inlier class D, if 

Qi = (1 − εi)Fi + εiHi, i ∈ N, (1) 

where Fi ∈ D is an inlier distribution, Hi is an arbitrary distribution and the proportion of contami-nation satisfies εi ≤ ε.

We consider inlier distributions with either exponentially- or polynomially-decaying tails. For univariate random variables, they are specified in Definitions 2 and 3, respectively. 

Definition 2 (Gθ,M class of distributions) . For M > 0 and 0 < θ ≤ 2, let Gθ,M denote the class of distributions on R satisfying that 

EW ∼P ∈G θ,M 

h

exp (|W − E W |/M )θ i

≤ 2.

This Gθ,M class consists of sub-Weibull distributions of order θ with the Orlicz ψθ-norm upper bounded by M , satisfying P(|W − E W | ≥ x) ≤ 2e−(x/M )θ

for any x > 0 and W ∼ F ∈ G θ,M . See Section S6.1 for details. 

Definition 3 (Pv,ϕ class of distributions) . For 0 < ϕ < ∞ and v ≥ 2, let Pv,ϕ denote the class of distributions on R satisfying that 

EW ∼P ∈P v,ϕ 

(|W − E W |/ϕ )v ≤ 1.

In other words, each distribution within the class Pv,ϕ has its absolute v-th central moment bounded above by ϕv and possesses a polynomially-decaying tail, as P(|W − E W | ≥ x) ≤ (ϕ/x )v for any x > 0and W ∼ F ∈ P v,ϕ , using Markov’s inequality. This is typically much heavier than an exponentially-decaying tail and thus more challenging statistically. Extending to multivariate settings, let Gpθ,M and Ppv,ϕ denote the classes of distributions on Rp

whose coordinates are independent, each with a marginal distribution belonging to Gθ,M and Pv,ϕ ,respectively. 

1.1.2 Online change point detection 

In view of the dynamic Huber contamination model defined in Definition 1, the change point setup is based on the inlier distribution sequence {Fi}i∈N, formalised in Assumption 1. 

Assumption 1. Assume that {Xi}i∈N is a sequence of independent random variables drawn from a dynamic Huber ε-contamination model with inlier class D, as defined in Definition 1. For i ∈ N, let 

fi = EY ∼Fi [Y ] be the mean of the inlier distribution Fi. Assume that there exists ∆ ∈ N ∪ {∞} such that 

f1 = · · · = f∆̸ = f∆+1 = f∆+2 = · · · .

When ∆̸ = ∞, let κ = ∥f∆ − f∆+1 ∥2 be the jump size. 

We write the probability of any event induced by any distribution satisfying Assumption 1 as P∆(·). An online change point procedure is characterised by an extended stopping time ˆ t ∈ N ∪ {∞} ,with respect to the natural filtration generated by the data. The false alarm probability of an online change point procedure is P∆(ˆ t < ∆), ∆ ∈ N ∪ {∞} . For ∆ < ∞, define the detection delay as (ˆ t − ∆) + = max {ˆt − ∆, 0}. We aim to develop a procedure that simultaneously controls the false alarm probability at a prescribed level α > 0, and guarantees that the detection delay (ˆ t − ∆) + is small for all ∆ < ∞.21.2 Summary of the results

1.3 Related literature 

1.3.1 Robust statistics 

Robust statistics aims to develop procedures that remain accurate when classical modelling assump-tions, such as Gaussianity or the absence of contamination, fail. Classical work primarily focused on the robustness properties of estimators in asymptotic frameworks. Prominent examples include Huber’s M-estimator (Huber, 1964) and depth-based estimators such as Tukey’s halfspace median (Tukey, 1975). While these estimators provide important conceptual foundations for robustness, some of them are computationally challenging in high dimensions. For instance, computing the halfspace median requires exponential time in the dimension in general. More recent work, particularly from theoretical computer science, has shifted the focus towards algorithms with simultaneous statistical and computational guarantees. Pioneered by Diakonikolas et al. (2016) and Lai et al. (2016), this line of work develops polynomial-time algorithms for robust mean estimation under strong contamination models, together with finite-sample error bounds. This modern perspective replaces purely asymptotic robustness guarantees with non-asymptotic rates that explicitly capture the effects of sample size, dimension, contamination level and moment assumptions. 3A central theme in this literature is the design of algorithms that can handle complex data. Many robust mean estimators output a weighted average of the observed points where weights are chosen to downweight or remove suspected outliers. Several approaches have been developed for this purpose, including covariance-based filtering methods (see Diakonikolas and Kane, 2023, for a review), semidef-inite programming approaches (e.g. Cheng et al., 2019), and non-convex optimization methods (e.g. Cheng et al., 2020). Other robust procedures that do not rely on explicit weighting schemes include M-estimators (e.g. Catoni, 2012), median-of-means methods (e.g. Lugosi and Mendelson, 2019b) and the trimmed mean (e.g. Lugosi and Mendelson, 2021). Modern work on robust mean estimation has led to a detailed study of different sources of distri-butional challenges: heavy-tailed inlier distributions and adversarial contamination. In the uncontam-inated setting, one line of work seeks estimators with sub-Gaussian performance under weak moment assumptions (e.g. Lugosi and Mendelson, 2019a,b; Hopkins et al., 2020). Under strong contamination, optimal estimation error rates have been established for several classes of inlier distributions, including Gaussian inliers with known covariance (e.g. Diakonikolas et al., 2016), sub-Gaussian inliers with un-known covariance, and distributions with bounded k-th moments for k ≥ 2 (e.g. Steinhardt et al., 2018; Diakonikolas et al., 2020). Importantly, these rates can be achieved by polynomial-time algorithms. Under the Huber contamination model, (e.g. Prasad et al., 2020) developed a mean estimator that is optimal under both heavy-tailed and contaminated models. Recent work has also studied robust estimation under additional structural assumptions, such as sparsity of the mean (e.g. Balakrishnan et al., 2017) or mean-shift contamination models (e.g. Kotekal and Gao, 2025; Diakonikolas et al., 2025). While there is extensive literature on robust mean estimation, existing finite-sample theory for robust mean testing remains largely concentrated on Gaussian-type models. Diakonikolas et al. (2017) was the first to study sample complexity bounds for testing whether the mean of a Huber-contaminated Gaussian distribution is zero or not, assuming known covariance. Building on this work, Canonne et al. (2023) derived tight sample complexity bounds under strong and weak contamination models. Other recent work has investigated testing with unknown covariance (e.g. Canonne et al., 2021; Diakonikolas et al., 2023) and under sparsity assumptions (e.g. George and Canonne, 2022). Substantially less is known for contaminated non-Gaussian settings. For heavy-tailed distributions without contamination, practical testing procedures were studied in, e.g. Chen and Qin (2010) and Wang et al. (2015), but only from an asymptotic perspective. Beyond mean estimation and testing, robustness has been studied in many other statistical prob-lems, including covariance estimation (e.g. Chen et al., 2018), covariance testing (e.g. Diakonikolas and Kane, 2021) and linear regression (see Yu and Yao, 2017, for a review). 

1.3.2 Change point detection analysis 

Change point detection problems are commonly classified into online and offline settings. In the online setting, observations arrive sequentially, and one must decide in real time whether a change point has occurred. The goal is therefore to minimise the delay in detecting a change point. In contrast, offline change point analysis is conducted retrospectively, using a fixed dataset observed over the entire time horizon. The aim is then to accurately estimate the number and locations of the change points. In offline change point detection, three main statistical goals are commonly studied, namely testing, localisation and inference. Testing concerns the problem of distinguishing a no-change model from alternatives containing at least one change point. Minimax testing rates have been derived in several settings under independent-coordinate assumptions, including Gaussian models (e.g. Liu et al., 2021; Verzelen et al., 2023), and heavy-tailed models (e.g. Li et al., 2026). Testing procedures have also been developed beyond the independent Gaussian setting, for example allowing dependence across coordinates or requiring weaker moment assumptions (e.g. Yu and Chen, 2021; Wang et al., 2022), though the type I error guarantees for these procedures are often asymptotic. Localisation aims to estimate the change point locations. As for the univariate mean change point setting, many methods have been proposed for localisation, including binary segmentation and its variants (e.g. Scott and Knott, 1974; Fryzlewicz, 2014; Kov´ acs et al., 2023), as well as penalisation-based methods (e.g. Jackson et al., 2005; Bleakley and Vert, 2011; Killick et al., 2012; Rojas and Wahlberg, 2014; Maidstone 4et al., 2017). Finally, inference concerns uncertainty quantification after detecting changes, including constructing confidence intervals for change point locations (e.g. Kaul et al., 2021; Wang and Shao, 2023; Xu et al., 2024; Xue et al., 2026), as well as confidence sets for the underlying piecewise constant mean function (e.g. Frick et al., 2014; Pein et al., 2017; Fang et al., 2020). Offline change point analysis has also generated a large body of methodological, computational, and theoretical work. Methodological developments address settings that go beyond the classical independent univariate model, including temporal dependence in the noise (e.g. Dehling et al., 2015; Wang et al., 2022; Cho and Fryzlewicz, 2024; Xu et al., 2024), high-dimensional settings (e.g. Dette and G¨ osmann, 2018; Wang and Samworth, 2018; Kaul et al., 2021) and missing data settings (Follain et al., 2022), to name but a few. From a computational perspective, recursive search procedures such as binary segmentation and its variants provide scalable alternatives to exhaustive search, while pruning strategies for penalised segmentation methods improve the efficiency of exact or near-exact optimisation. On the theoretical side, a central goal is to characterise the optimal localisation error and the minimal signal strength required for consistent localisation, often within a minimax framework. For instance, Wang et al. (2020) established minimax localisation rates in the univariate setting and showed that optimal partitioning and wild binary segmentation attain these rates under suitable conditions. Multivariate extensions have been considered in Verzelen et al. (2023) and Pilliat et al. (2023). Online mean change point detection has analogous goals, but with additional constraints arising from the sequential nature of the data. The first goal is sequential detection, where the aim is to declare a change as soon as possible while controlling the frequency of false alarms. Classical sequential analysis often formulates this through the average run length in the absence of change (Lorden, 1971). This criterion underlies much of the early work for CUSUM and generalised likelihood-ratio (GLR) procedures (e.g. Page, 1954; Lai, 1995; Mei, 2006; Xie and Siegmund, 2013). Among detectors with average run length control, Moustakides (1986) proved that the CUSUM procedure of Page (1954) is optimal under the detection-delay benchmark of Lorden (1971). A second line of work controls the overall probability of ever raising an alarm over the monitoring horizon (Chu et al., 1996). In recent years, this framework has attracted increasing attention, where both GLR- and CUSUM-based procedures are studied under this paradigm (e.g. Dette and G¨ osmann, 2020; Chen et al., 2022; G¨ osmann et al., 2022; Yu et al., 2023). Within the class of procedures satisfying false-alarm-probability control, Yu et al. (2023) showed that, for univariate sub-Gaussian observations, a CUSUM procedure based on the sample mean achieves minimax optimal detection delay. The second goal is post-detection inference, where the objective is to quantify uncertainty about the location of the change after an alarm has been raised. For example, Chen et al. (2024) studied the construction of confidence intervals for the actual change point location in the online framework. The online setting brings computational considerations to the forefront. Since observations arrive sequentially, practical procedures must often have low update and storage costs. Recent work has therefore developed more efficient methods for evaluating generalised likelihood ratios (e.g. Romano et al., 2023; Ward et al., 2024; Pishchagina et al., 2026), as well as grid-based search strategies that reduce update and storage costs (Chen et al., 2022; Moen, 2025). Although our focus is on mean changes, change point analysis has been studied for many other forms of structural changes. These include changes in covariance structure (e.g. Aue et al., 2009; Avanesov and Buzun, 2018; Wang et al., 2020; Li and Li, 2023), regression coefficients (e.g. Horv´ ath et al., 2004; Lee et al., 2016; Rinaldo et al., 2021) and beyond; see Yu (2020) for a recent review. 

1.3.3 Robust change point detection analysis 

A growing body of work has incorporated robustness into mean change point algorithms, primarily in the offline setting. To reduce the effect of outliers or contaminated observations, one approach is to replace the squared-error loss in penalised cost formulations by the Huber loss (Fearnhead and Rigaill, 2019). Another approach is to incorporate robust mean estimators into CUSUM-based procedures (Li and Yu, 2021). Robustness can also be achieved through nonparametric methods, many of which are designed to detect general distributional shifts, and are therefore less sensitive to heavy-tailed observations. In high-dimensional settings, U-statistic-based methods have been used to detect change 5points in distributions with undefined means (Yu and Chen, 2022), as well as in data with heavy-tailed and mildly dependent errors (Jiang et al., 2023; Xu et al., 2024). In the online setting, existing robust detectors (see, e.g. Moustakides, 1985; Unnikrishnan et al., 2011; Cao and Xie, 2017) primarily focus on sequential testing under parametric uncertainty. For example, in a Gaussian model with unknown mean and known variance, their null and alternative hypotheses specify that the mean belongs to one or two known convex sets. In contrast, our set-ting involves both unknown inlier means before and after the change, and observations drawn from Huber-contaminated versions of the corresponding inlier distributions. This distinction motivates the development of new methodology. Beyond mean change point models, robustness has also been considered in stump models (Mukher-jee et al., 2022), covariance change point problems (Ramsay and Chenouri, 2021) and high-dimensional linear regression coefficient changes (see, e.g. Liu et al., 2023; Cho and Owens, 2024; Xu et al., 2024; Zhao et al., 2024). These developments highlight the broad relevance of robustness in change point analysis, while also underscoring that online mean change point detection under Huber contamination remains comparatively less explored. 

1.4 Notation 

We introduce the notation used throughout the paper. Let N denote the set of positive integers. For 

d ∈ N, write [ d] = {1, . . . , d }. Let ⌈·⌉ , ⌊·⌋ and Γ( ·) denote the ceiling, floor and Gamma functions, respectively. For a set S, use 1S and |S| to denote its indicator function and cardinality respectively. For a sequence of observations {Xt}t∈N, denote the sub-sequence as Xs:t = ( Xs, . . . , X t). For two sequences of positive real numbers {an} and {bn}, we write an ≲ bn (or bn ≳ an) if there exists a constant C > 0 such that an ≤ Cb n for all n sufficiently large. We write an ≍ bn if both 

an ≲ bn and an ≳ bn hold. In the proofs of results in Sections 3 and 4, we write an ≲log bn if there exists a constant k ≥ 0 such that an ≲ bn log k(bn). The notation an ≍log bn is defined accordingly. Throughout the paper, O(·) denotes bounds up to universal multiplicative constants, while ˜O(·) ad-ditionally suppresses logarithmic factors. Unless stated otherwise, these implicit constants, as well as those denoted by C, C 1, C 2, . . . , are understood to depend only on the parameters α, v, M, θ and ϕ,which are treated as fixed. 

# 2 Univariate change point detection: Minimax rates and optimal procedures 

In this section, we present a comprehensive analysis of the univariate case. We first establish lower bounds on the detection delay for all regimes in Tables 1 and 2, which characterise the fundamental statistical difficulty of robust online change point detection under contamination and heavy-tailed noise. We then propose a robust online detection procedure, based on a combination of the median and the robust univariate mean estimator (RUME, Prasad et al., 2020), and prove that it is minimax optimal, up to logarithmic factors.

Theorem 1 identifies a series of phase transitions in the robust univariate online change point detec-tion problems. As the signal strength increases, the principal statistical bottleneck shifts successively from the change point location, to the tail behaviour, and finally to the contamination level. In Regime 1, no change point estimator is guaranteed to detect the change point. More precisely, when the jump size is too small relative either to the contamination level, or to the change point location, no procedure can reliably detect the change point. Regime 1a reflects a contamination-driven impossibility. The adversarial component can mask the mean shift effectively, such that consistent detection is impossible. Regime 1b captures a different obstruction. Even without contamination, if the change occurs too early relative to the signal size, then the available pre-change sample is insufficient to localise the change with non-trivial accuracy; see Moen (2025, Proposition 8). Once the signal exceeds this detectability threshold, the problem enters Regime 2, where the detection delay exhibits the familiar sub-Gaussian-type scaling σ2κ−2 log(1 /α ) (Yu et al., 2023). In this regime, the signal is strong enough to be detectable, but not yet so that robustness or tail effects dominate. Consequently, the delay is governed primarily by the number of observations required to average out the intrinsic noise in the inlier distribution. Regime 3 marks the onset of genuinely heavy-tail behaviour. Further increases in the signal no longer translate into the Gaussian rate of improvement as in Regime 2. Instead, the detection delay is controlled by the tail decay of the inlier distribution. For sub-Weibull inliers, the lower bound scales as κ−θ log(1 /α ), and ( v log( κ)) −1 log(1 /α ) for the finite-vth-moment class. Intuitively, although the signal is now large, extreme inlier observations can dominate the detection delay. In Regime 4, the contamination level becomes the dominant constraint. Beyond this point, in-creasing the signal strength no longer yields a corresponding reduction in the detection delay. The delay is lower bounded by a quantity of order log(1 /α )/ log((1 − ε)/ε ), reflecting the intrinsic difficulty of separating contaminated from uncontaminated observations in an online setting. This regime may therefore be viewed as the contamination-limited phase. Taken together, these four regimes provide a unified picture of the minimax difficulty of the uni-variate problem by precisely characterising the qualitatively distinct phenomena, depending on the relative magnitudes of the jump size, contamination proportion and tail parameters. 82.2 A robust online change point detection procedure 

In this subsection, we develop a robust online change point detection procedure for univariate data. This procedure is designed to retain power under both Huber contamination and heavy-tailed inlier noise. It follows the standard online mean change point detection routine. For a sequence {Xt}t∈N ⊆ R

and for a mean estimator bμs:t based on data Xs:t, we declare a change point at t ∈ N, if max  

> 1≤s< ⌊t/ 2⌋

bμ1: s − bμ(s+1): t

exceeds a prescribed threshold. This type of procedure has been incorporated in many recent algo-rithms; see e.g. Cho (2016), Liu et al. (2021), Follain et al. (2022), Yu et al. (2023), Zhang et al. (2023) and Gong et al. (2024). In view of the contamination and heavy-tailedness, we summon some robust mean estimators as choices for bμ. In particular, we consider both medians and the robust univariate mean estimator (RUME, Prasad et al., 2020), depending on the available sample size. The algorithm is detailed in Algorithm 1. To be specific, for a pair of integers s, t satisfying ht ≤ s ≤ ⌊ t/ 2⌋ and 2 |s, where ht is some threshold (to be specified) that depends on t, we consider bμs:t = RUME( Xs:t), otherwise bμs:t = median( Xs:t). This means that employing the RUME estimator only when the available sample is sufficient, as required by the theoretical guarantees to be shown in Lemmas S29 and S30. The thresholds {χs,t } and {ζs,t } are parametrised by the contamination level and the tail behaviour of the inliers. The theoretical choices for these thresholds will be presented in Theorems 2 and 3 below, with practical guidance discussed in Section 5.1. We note that the algorithm requires the contamination level ε and the tail behaviour of the inlier distribution as inputs. These are commonly seen in the robust statistics literature; see e.g. Cheng et al. (2019), Prasad et al. (2020) and Li and Yu (2021) for the former, and e.g. Comminges et al. (2021) and Li et al. (2026) for the latter. In practice, tail parameters may be estimated from pilot data when available (see, e.g. Hill, 1975; Vladimirova et al., 2020), or calibrated under a conservative tail assumption otherwise, for example by assuming only finite variance. In Section 6 we provide some further discussions on relaxation of the requirement on ε.

In the no-change regime ∆ = ∞, Theorem 2( a) shows that the false alarm probability is controlled at level α. Moreover, Theorem 2( b) ensures that, even when a change is present, the procedure does not stop before the change point with probability at least 1 − α. Algorithm 1 thus controls false alarm uniformly over both the null and pre-change segments. More importantly, Theorem 2 provides high-probability upper bounds on the detection delay under exponentially decaying tails, and these bounds match the lower bounds in Theorem 1, up to a factor of log(∆). Under Assumption 2( a), corresponding to the moderate-signal regime, the delay is of order 

κ−2 log(∆ /α ) when the contamination level is not too severe. In this range, the RUME-based com-ponent of the procedure drives detection, and the rate is the same Gaussian-type scaling in Regime 2 of the lower bound. With a larger signal strength, the delay in Theorem 2( c) is instead of the order log(∆ /α ), reflecting the sample size required for RUME to operate reliably under contamination. This illustrates that RUME recovers optimal rates only when detecting changes of moderate signal sizes. Under Assumption 2( b), the theorem enters the large-signal regime. In this range, the median-based component of the procedure drives detection. When κ is of order at least O(1) but still satisfies 

κ ≲ log 1/θ (1 /ε ), Theorem 2( d) shows that the detection delay is of the order κ−θ log(∆ /α ), corre-sponding to Regime 3 of the lower bound. This reveals explicitly how the sub-Weibull parameter θ

influences the detection problem: fixing the signal strength, the delay increases as θ decreases. Once the signal is sufficiently strong, the remaining difficulty is determined by the heaviness of the inlier tails rather than by Gaussian-type averaging. When κ ≳ log 1/θ (1 /ε ), the delay no longer improves with the jump size and is instead of order (log(1 /ε )) −1 log(∆ /α ). This behaviour is consistent with Regime 4 of the lower bound. At this point, detection is limited by the sample needed to observe a sufficient number of uncontaminated samples, rather than by any lack of separations between the pre- and post-change means. 10 2.2.2 Detection guarantees under finite-moment assumptions

By choosing thresholds χs,t and ζs,t adapted to the finite-moment class, Algorithm 1 again achieves the same false alarm guarantees as in Theorem 2. When ∆ = ∞, the false alarm probability is controlled at level α; when a change is present, the procedure does not stop before the change point with probability at least 1 − α.Theorem 3 also provides high-probability upper bounds on the detection delay under finite-moment assumptions, and these bounds match the lower bounds of Theorem 1, up to a factor of log(∆). The main distinction compared to the results under sub-Weibull assumptions lies in how the tail behaviour influences the rates. Under Assumption 3( a), corresponding to the moderate-signal regime, the delay retains the Gaussian-type scaling κ−2 log(∆ /α ) under mild contamination. However, under Assump-tion 3( b), corresponding to the large-signal regime, the dependence on the number of moments v be-comes explicit. Specifically, when κ = O(1) and κ ≲ ε−1/v , the delay scales as ( v log( κ)) −1 log(∆ /α ), showing that heavier tails slow detection relative to the Gaussian case. Once κ ≳ ε−1/v , the delay 11 saturates at order (log(1 /ε )) −1 log(∆ /α ), reflecting the need to accumulate sufficiently many uncon-taminated observations. Putting Theorems 1, 2 and 3 together, we obtain a complete characterisation of the rates of detection delay for the univariate change point problem, up to logarithmic factors. We note that the lower bound in Theorem 1 is stated in terms of the expected detection delay, while Theorems 2 and 3 provide high-probability upper bounds. This discrepancy is commonly seen in the literature, and recent works (e.g. Chhor et al., 2025; Ma et al., 2026) have shown remedies for unification under weak conditions. 

# 3 Robust multivariate mean testing algorithms 

In this section, we develop a robust multivariate mean testing procedure, which will serve as the main subroutine in our change point detection algorithm. This strategy is motivated by viewing change point detection as a sequence of tests for equality of mean vectors between two subsamples. Rather than estimating the pre- and post-change means and then comparing the resulting estimates, as in our univariate procedure, we directly test whether the corresponding mean vectors are equal. This distinction is relevant in the multivariate setting, where testing and estimation may exhibit different sample-size requirements; see, e.g. the introduction sections of Diakonikolas et al. (2017) and Canonne et al. (2023) for further discussion. We consider the robust mean testing problem under the dynamic Huber ε-contamination model (1). Given a sample Y = {Y1, . . . , Y n} ⊆ Rp, we assume that the data are generated independently accord-ing to 

Yi ∼ (1 − ε)F + εH i, (7) where F ∈ D represents the inlier distribution with mean μ, ∥μ∥2 = κ, and identity covariance I. The contamination distributions Hi’s are arbitrary and may vary with i ∈ [n]. In this section, we consider the hypothesis testing problem H0 : μ = 0 vs. H1 : μ̸ = 0. (8) This problem is well understood in the Gaussian context. In particular, suppose that F = Np(μ, I )and the alternative is written as ∥μ∥2 ≥ κmin . In the absence of contamination, i.e. when ε = 0, the minimax testing rate satisfies κ2min ≍log 

√p/n (e.g. Baraud, 2002; Ingster and Suslina, 2003). Under contamination, Canonne et al. (2023) established minimax lower and upper bounds of the form 

√pn + ε3/2

r pn ≲ κ2min ≲

√pn + ε

r pn + ε2 log(1 /ε ).

To the best of our knowledge, finite-sample analyses have yet been established for the contaminated setting in (7), where the inlier class D may contain heavy-tailed distributions. To address the testing problem in (8), we propose robust procedures for two classes of heavy-tailed inlier distributions: the sub-Weibull class Gpθ,M and the finite-moment class Ppv,ϕ . The algorithms and theoretical guarantees for each class are presented in Sections 3.1 and 3.2, respectively. The procedures build upon the soft-filter framework developed in Section 8 of Canonne et al. (2023). While Canonne et al. (2023) analyse this framework for Np(μ, I ) inliers, we extend the analysis to accommodate heavy-tailed inliers. 

3.1 Robust testing for the sub-Weibull class 

We first develop a robust mean testing procedure, Algorithm 2, for the sub-Weibull inlier class Gpθ,M .In a nutshell, Algorithm 2 uses filtering-based algorithms to downweight the suspected outliers, and then rejects or accepts the null hypothesis based on the weighted sum of the observations. The filtering mechanism here consists of two steps. First, a spectral filtering procedure (Al-gorithm S1 or S2, depending on whether the sample size is smaller than the number of dimen-sions) iteratively downweights observations by examining the leading eigenvector of M (w, Y) − nI 

12 or Gram( w, Y) − nI , where

are the second moment matrix and Gram matrix respectively. Observations with more extreme pro-jections along this leading eigenvector are more likely to be outliers or possess heavy tails, and are therefore downweighted more. Second, the weights are further refined using Algorithm S3, which removes the un observations with the largest deviation scores τk = √wkYk, Sum( w, Y) − wkp ,where Sum( w, A) = P

⟨Sum( w, Y), Sum( w, B)⟩ that arises from expanding the test statistic in (S53), where B is the collec-tion of contaminated samples or those with extreme behaviour. This procedure is an instance of the filtering-based methods commonly used in robust statistics. The objective is to construct weights that preserve the signal contributed by the inliers while reducing the influence of contaminated observations (see, e.g. Lai et al., 2016; Diakonikolas et al., 2016, 2017; Cheng et al., 2019). Further details of Algorithm 2 are collected in Section S3.2. The theoretical choices for the algorithmic thresholds are discussed next in Theorem 4.

Theorem 4 provides finite-sample guarantees for Algorithm 2, under sub-Weibull inliers and Huber contamination. It shows that Algorithm 2, which runs in polynomial time, controls both type I and II errors, provided the input signal size κ0 is appropriately chosen. The input κ0 is the signal size used in the testing procedure to set the rejection threshold. Under the null hypothesis, κ0 should be large enough so the threshold is above the statistical fluctuation of the test statistics. Under the alternative, the true signal strength κ should satisfy the lower bound in (10), and κ0 is required to be of the same order as κ.The condition 2 /C γ ≤ κ20/κ 2 ≤ ¯c has two purposes. The upper bound ensures that the rejection threshold is not too large compared with the true signal, so that the signal can still be detected. Similar upper bound requirements are also observed in the change point literature (e.g. Chen et al., 2022; Madrid Padilla et al., 2022). The lower bound ensures that the filtering radius Rf in Algorithm 2 is large enough to contain the typical fluctuations in the operator norm of the empirical second-moment matrix or Gram matrix. This is an artefact of the proof and a potential relaxation is discussed in Remark S3. The condition (9) on the de facto outlier level u ensures that the combined proportion of con-taminated observations and extreme inlier observations is controlled with high probability. Similar small-contamination conditions are used in robust estimation and testing; see, e.g. Prasad et al. (2020) and Lugosi and Mendelson (2021). The theoretical choice of the detection sensitivity factor is Tu = (1 − 7u)2 − 2u. This factor appears in the rejection threshold and corresponds to a worst-case lower bound on the total weight retained on the set of inlier points close to the mean after filtering under the alternative. In practice, the filtering subroutines, GramFilter or MomentFilter, typically retain a much larger fraction of these weights. Thus, in Section 5, we use a calibrated form Tu = (1 − ku )2, where k is chosen empirically to ensure that the observed false alarm rate does not exceed α.The minimum signal strength requirement in (10) consists of three terms. The first term, √p/n ,is the classical Gaussian mean testing rate. The second term, ε2p log(1 /ε ), reflects the effect of Huber contamination on the filtering step. The third term p/n 2 arises from controlling the second-moment matrix under sub-Weibull inliers. It is useful to compare this with the Gaussian strong contamination setting studied by Canonne et al. (2023). Their results imply a minimum signal strength requirement of the form 

κ2 ≳

 √pn + ε2 log(1 /ε ) + ε

r pn



· polylog( p, n, 1/ε, 1/δ ). (11) When √p/n is the dominating term in both (10) and (11), Theorem 4 recovers the Gaussian result. In other regimes, our bound has an additional factor of p in the contamination-related term, as well as an extra p/n 2 term. This discrepancy arises from our use of the matrix Bernstein inequality (Theorem S41) to control the truncated second-moment matrix. The analysis of Canonne et al. (2023), who considered only the Gaussian setting, uses an ε-net argument to obtain sharper concentration of the second-moment matrix. This approach, although leading to optimal rates in the Gaussian setting, would incur an additional factor of p1/θ in the final rates under the sub-Weibull setting. 

3.2 Robust testing for the finite-moment class 

When Algorithm 2 is used under the sub-Weibull assumption, i.e. D = Gpθ,M , the minimum required signal strength to control type I and II errors depends only logarithmically on the failure probabil-ity. In contrast, when the same procedure is used under finite-moment assumptions, i.e. D = Ppv,ϕ ,the corresponding signal strength requirement depends polynomially on the failure probability; see Theorem S14. While this is not a major issue for the stand-alone mean testing problem when the 14 failure probability is treated as a fixed constant, it becomes problematic when Algorithm 2 is applied repeatedly for online change point detection. Distributing the type I error budget across many tests would make the minimum detection threshold needed for non-trivial power grow quickly over time. To address this issue, in Algorithm 3, we use a median-of-means-based approach. We note that median-of-means-type statistics have been applied in a wide range of statistical problems (see, e.g. Lerasle and Oliveira, 2011; Lugosi and Mendelson, 2019b; Hopkins et al., 2020; Lecu´ e and Lerasle, 2020; Li et al., 2026). To be specific, we split the sample into K blocks, run Algorithm 2 on each block and aggregate the resulting statistics by their median. This turns constant-probability block-level control into high-probability control with only logarithmic dependence on the failure probability. We reject the null when this median is large. The full procedure is presented in Algorithm 3.

Theorem 5 gives finite-sample guarantees on type I and II error rates for Algorithm 3 under finite-moment inliers. Similar to Theorem 4, a large enough κ0 is needed to control the type I error. To control type II error, κ0 must be of the same order as the true signal strength κ, with κ satisfying (13). The replacement of condition (9) by (12) reflects the need to control the proportion of contaminated observations and extreme-valued inliers for each group for the median-of-means construction. The median-of-means step is used to obtain high-probability control. Each block only needs a constant-probability guarantee, and taking the median across K = ⌈8 log(1 /ω )⌉ blocks amplifies this to a failure probability of at most ω. As a result, the signal requirement depends only logarithmically on 1 /ω , which is essential for the online change point settings in Section 4. When n > pK , the minimum signal strength requirement in (13) consists of the Gaussian test-ing term √p/n , the contamination term ε2p log(1 /ε ), and two finite moment terms ( p/n )2−2/v +

p1+ v/ 2/n v/ 2. The latter two terms reflect the cost of controlling second-moment fluctuations under only a finite v-th moment assumption, decreasing with increasing v. Compared with the Gaussian result of Canonne et al. (2023), Theorem 5 recovers the same leading rate when √p/n dominates. The additional terms quantify the price of finite-moment inliers and the matrix concentration argu-ment used after truncation. When n ≤ pK , the minimum signal strength requirement is of order p,independent of n. This could be an artefact of the proof; see Remark S4 for detailed explanations. The runtime of Algorithm 3 is essentially K = ⌈8 log(1 /ω )⌉ times that of Algorithm 2 applied to a block of size n0. Thus, the algorithm still runs in polynomial time. 

# 4 Robust multivariate online change point detection 

In this section, we apply the robust mean testing algorithms developed in Section 3 to the online change point detection problem. The resulting procedure is given in Algorithm 4. Its theoretical properties are studied in Theorems 6 and 7, for the sub-Weibull class Gpθ,M and the finite-moment class Ppv,ϕ , respectively. The main idea of Algorithm 4 is to reduce the online change point detection problem to a sequence of robust mean testing problems. At each t ≥ 2, we compare observations from the beginning and the end of the current data stream by forming pairwise differences 

Yr,t = Xt−r+1 − Xr

√2σ , r = 1 , . . . , ⌊t/ 2⌋.

This construction, also used in Li et al. (2026), transforms a possible change in the mean of {Xt} into a non-zero mean in the derived variables {Yr,t }. Under the no-change model, these variables have zero inlier means. Under the alternative, the inlier mean is non-zero for a range of r; see Theorem S17 for details. Algorithm 4 scans over time t and candidate window sizes s. For each pair ( s, t ) satisfying the minimum sample size requirement, the procedure applies the appropriate robust mean testing algo-rithm to the derived sample {Yi,t }si=1 . Depending on the tail behaviour of the data, we apply either Algorithm 2 or its median-of-means variant, Algorithm 3. We note that this choice depends on the knowledge of the tail properties of data points. The procedure stops as soon as one of these tests rejects the null hypothesis. We now state the signal size conditions under which Algorithm 4 achieves false alarm control and detection delay guarantees. 

Assumption 4. We assume that one of the following holds.

Assumption 4 is the change point analogue of the mean testing signal conditions in Section 3. In particular, Assumption 4( a) corresponds to (10) for sub-Weibull inliers, and Assumption 4( b) to (13) for finite-moment inliers. This connection arises because, after a change occurs at time ∆, the pairwise differences used in Assumption 4 form a mean testing problem with signal size proportional to κ and sample size of order ∆. The extra logarithmic factors come from repeated testing over many times and window sizes, since the total false alarm probability α is split across all tests. When the term √p/ ∆ dominates, Assumption 4 reduces, up to logarithmic factors, to κ2∆ ≳log 

√p. This matches the rate-optimal signal strength requirements in multivariate online change point detection (e.g. Proposition 8 in Moen, 2025). Theorems 6 and 7 provide theoretical guarantees of Algorithm 4 for inlier distributions Gpθ,M and 

Ppv,ϕ , respectively. Firstly, Algorithm 4 achieves false alarm control analogous to Theorems 2 and 3. When there is no change point (∆ = ∞), the false alarm probability is at most α, while in the presence of a change point, the procedure does not stop before the change with probability at least 1 − α.Secondly, when there is a change and Assumption 4 holds, the detection delay of Algorithm 4 can be controlled. Similar to Regimes 2–4 in Tables 1 and 2 for the univariate setting, three distinct detection delay regimes emerge in the multivariate case once the change becomes detectable. For relatively small signal strengths satisfying κ ≳ εpp log(1 /ε ), the detection delay scales as √p/κ 2.This rate corresponds to Regime 2 in the univariate setting, which is recovered by setting p = 1. It also matches the expected detection delay in the online setting under Gaussian assumptions for dense changes obtained in Chen et al. (2022). As κ increases further, the improvement in detection delay slows down: for sub-Weibull inliers, the rate becomes √p/κ , while for inliers with only finite v-th moment, the rate becomes 

p1+2 /v 

κ4/v + pκv/ (v−1) .

18 We conjecture that this slower decay rate reflects the increased difficulty of robust detection under heavy-tailed distributions, motivated by the observations from Regime 3 of the univariate setting. Finally, in the strong-signal regime where κ ≳ √p, the detection delay becomes ˜O(1). This saturation is not caused by a lack of signal strength, but rather by the minimum sample requirements needed to guarantee the statistical accuracy of the mean tests under contamination and heavy-tailed noise; see (9) and (12). We conjecture that this corresponds to Regime 4 in the univariate setting, where the detection delay is also of constant order up to logarithmic factors. The rates for the intermediate and large κ regimes may not be tight, as they follow from the mean testing analysis. To further illustrate the above result, we briefly compare with the relevant literature, although scarce. Li et al. (2026) studied the minimax testing problem for offline high-dimensional mean change point detection. Their main results suggest that when the change is dense the minimax testing rates are essentially unaffected by the tail behaviour of the underlying distribution. This message is consistent with our detection-delay guarantees in the small κ regime, where the delay is not affected by the heavy-tailed noise compared to the Gaussian case. We note that the parameter choices stated in Theorems 6 and 7 are designed to transfer the type I error and power guarantees of the robust mean testing subroutines to the change point procedure. In particular, the minimum sample size threshold ( ht)t∈N is chosen according to (14) and (15), which are the minimum sample sizes for Algorithms 2 and 3 to detect a mean change of magnitude κ with type I and II error control under Gpθ,M and Ppv,ϕ , respectively. Thus, whenever a test is performed, the corresponding robust mean testing subroutine is applied with a sufficient sample size. Practical guidance on choosing the thresholds in (14) and (15) is given in Section 5.3. 

# 5 Simulation studies 

We present simulation results to empirically validate our theoretical predictions. Specifically, we investigate the empirical performance of Algorithm 1 for univariate online change point detection (Section 5.1), Algorithm 4 with a slight variant for multivariate online change point detection (Sec-tion 5.3) and Algorithm 2 for multivariate mean testing (Section 5.2). The code for reproducing our experiments can be found at https://github.com/edwintang903/online-robustcpt .

5.1 Univariate online change point detection 

In this subsection, we run simulations to illustrate the four regimes of detection delay as presented in Tables 1 and 2, using the proposed online change point detection procedure Algorithm 1. We consider 

n = 2400 independent observations {Xi}ni=1 in R arriving sequentially. For each i ∈ { 1, . . . , n }, the observation is drawn from the distribution Qi defined in (1), with the contamination proportion set to be ε = 0 .1. The outlier distribution Hi is chosen to be a high-variance normal distribution N (0 , 100) and we consider the following two choices for the inlier distribution Fi:

• Laplace( μ, b = √10 .5): a Laplace distribution with location parameter μ and scale parameter 

b = √10 .5, so that the variance is σ2 = 2 b2 = 21; 

• t(μ, σ = √21 , ν = 2 .1): a rescaled Student’s t-distribution with ν = 2 .1 degrees of freedom, mean μ and variance σ2 = 21. Specifically, if Z ∼ tν , we have X = σ

q ν−2 

> ν

Z + μ ∼ t(μ, σ, ν ). These two distributions represent distinct heavy-tailed classes with the same variance: specifically, Laplace( μ, √10 .5) ∈ G 1,√10 .5 and t(μ, √21 , 2.1) ∈ P 2,21 . Using these distributions allows us to examine how detection delay varies under different tail behaviours. We set the desired false alarm probability to be α = 0 .2. In Algorithm 1, we use the detection thresholds χs,t and ζs,t specified in (3) and (4), respectively, for t(μ, √21 , 2.1) ∈ P 2,21 and the thresholds specified in (5) and (6) for Laplace( μ, √10 .5) ∈ G 1,√10 .5. Note that for χs,t , in (3) and (5), we replace M

and ϕ, respectively, by tunable constants to reduce the conservativeness of the procedure in practice. We then calibrate all constants appearing in these thresholds under the no-change setting so that the empirical false alarm rate is bounded by α = 0 .2. 19 To empirically evaluate the performance of Algorithm 1 under a change, we set a single change point at ∆ = 600, where the mean fi of the inlier distribution Fi undergoes a jump of size κ:

fi =

(

0 if i ≤ ∆,κ if i > ∆.

For each jump size κ in a suitably chosen grid, we conduct 2000 simulation runs to estimate the detection rate and the average detection delay. The detection rate denotes the probability that the detector raises an alarm after ∆ but before the arrival of the last observation. For the Laplace distribution, the regime transitions are illustrated in Figure 1: panel (a) shows the detection rate as a function of κ for small jump sizes, while panels (b) and (c) plot the mean detection delay against κ for moderate and large jump sizes, respectively. Analogous plots for the t-distribution are presented in Figure 2. These figures empirically verify our theoretical results and reveal all four regimes for detection delay as shown in Tables 1 and 2, for inlier distributions with exponentially- and polynomially-decaying tails, as we discuss in the following. As shown in Figures 1a and 2a, when κ = 0, the empirical false alarm rate is controlled at the desired level 0 .2. For small signal-to-noise ratios, specifically κ/σ ≤ 0.03 for Laplace( μ, √10 .5) and 

κ/σ ≤ 0.01 for t(μ, √21 , 2.1), the empirical probability of raising an alarm during the monitoring period is the same as the false alarm rate under no change. This indicates that, in this regime, the change point is effectively undetectable using our procedure, in agreement with Regime 1 of Tables 1 and 2. As κ/σ increases, the detection problem becomes easier, and the empirical non-detection probability decreases towards zero. Although the theoretical transition between Regimes 1 and 2 is sharp, the finite monitoring horizon results in a more gradual transition in the non-detection rate being observed in practice. Nevertheless, the phase transition behaviour remains clearly visible in the simulations. Figures 1b and 2b illustrate a transition in the detection decay dynamics between Regimes 2 and 3. The key observation is that no single relationship between the mean detection delay and the jump size provides a good fit across the entire range of medium jump strength. For smaller values of κ/σ ,an approximate inverse square law fits the data points, but this is no longer the case when the signal becomes larger. Towards the right part of both figures, we observe that the fit depends on the noise distribution: an inverse relationship d ∝ 1/κ for the Laplace distribution, and an inverse-logarithmic trend d ∝ 1/ log( κ) for the t-distribution. These observations agree with the order of the detection delay for Regimes 2 and 3 in Tables 1 and 2. As we increase κ/σ further above 1, both Figures 1c and 2c demonstrate that the detection delay of our procedure eventually plateaus and no longer depends on the jump size, which agrees with Regime 4 in both tables.

• (Laplace( μ, 1/√2)) ⊗p,

By using these distributions, we can quantify the difficulty of mean testing with respect to varying tail behaviours. Parameters for the distributions were chosen so that both distributions have variance 1. The remaining algorithmic inputs are chosen as follows. The desired type I error level is set to

5.2.1 Sensitivity analysis 

We first evaluate the robustness of the algorithm to different choices of the signal size input κ0 and filtering strength Cγ . We consider n = 500 samples with dimension p = {10 , 600 }. Recall that the filtering subroutine used in Algorithm 2 depends on whether n > p . Specifically, when p = 10, we use Algorithm S1, whereas when p = 600, we use Algorithm S2. For each value of κ0 ∈ { 0.1, 0.2, . . . , 2.5}

and Cγ = {0.01 , 0.03 , 0.05 , 0.07 , 0.1, 0.15 , 0.2}, we run Algorithm 2 on 1000 simulated datasets to obtain the empirical type I error rate and power. To compute the type I error, we set μ = 0 and record the proportion of false rejections. To evaluate the power, we fix μ = (1 /√p)1p, corresponding to a true signal strength κ = 1, and compute the proportion of correct rejections. In Figure 3, we overlay the type I error and power profiles for different values of Cγ in a single panel to aid comparison. The corresponding faceted plots are provided in Figure S4 of Section S5.2, where the same trends are shown separately for each value of Cγ .Across most settings in Figure 3, the type I error rate decreases rapidly to zero as κ0 increases from zero, whereas the power remains close to one over a wider range of κ0 and begins to decrease only when κ0 is close to, or exceeds, the true signal size κ = 1. This contrast is primarily driven by the role of κ0 in the rejection threshold. When κ0 is small, the threshold is low, so rejection is easy under both the null and the alternative, which leads to both high type I error and high power. As κ0

increases, the threshold becomes more stringent, reducing false rejections and hence lowering the type I error. Under the alternative, however, the non-zero mean inflates the test statistic, so the power remains high until the threshold becomes sufficiently large. For small values of Cγ , namely Cγ ∈ { 0.01 , 0.03 } in Figure 3b and Figure 3c, the behaviour differs from the general trend described above. When Cγ = 0 .01, both the type I error rate and the power are essentially zero throughout the range κ0 ∈ [0 .1, 2.5]. This is because a smaller Cγ makes the filtering step too aggressive, causing nearly all weights to be set to zero. As a result, the test statistic is unable to trigger rejection. When Cγ = 0 .03, the power curve exhibits a bell-shaped pattern centred near the true signal size κ = 1. This behaviour is due to the term Cγ nκ 20 in the filtering radius Rf

in Algorithm 2. For small κ0, the filtering radius is too small, so signal-bearing observations are filtered out too aggressively. As κ0 increases, the radius becomes large enough to retain enough of these observations, and the power increases. For even larger κ0, the rejection threshold becomes too stringent, and the power decreases for the same reason discussed above. The effect of Cγ itself is also clear from the plots. Increasing Cγ shifts both the type I error and power curves to the right. This is because a larger Cγ widens the filtering radius, allowing data points to retain larger weights and thereby inflating the test statistic under both the null and the alternative. Consequently, a larger κ0 is required both to control the type I error under the null and to induce a decay in power under the alternative. Once Cγ is sufficiently large, for example Cγ ≥ 0.07, the type I error and power profiles effectively overlap. This suggests that the filtering radius is then large enough for the spectral filtering step to become effectively inactive, so further increases in Cγ have little impact on the empirical behaviour of the test. These observations are consistent with Propositions 4 and S14, which impose two requirements on the choice of κ0: it must be sufficiently large for type I error control, and it must be of the same scale as the true signal size κ for power control. Under Cγ = 0 .1, panels (a), (b), and (c) suggest that choosing κ0 ∈ [0 .8, 1.4] achieves both type I error control and high power, while panel (d) suggests that choosing κ0 ∈ [1 , 2.5] also achieves both. Motivated by these findings, we set Cγ = 0 .1 for the remaining simulations. 23 (a) Laplace distribution, p = 10 , ε = 0 .01. (b) t-distribution, p = 10 , ε = 0 .01.         

> (c) Laplace distribution, p= 600 , ε = 0 .01. (d) t-distribution, p= 600 , ε = 0 .01.

Figure 3: Empirical type I error and power in testing between hypotheses in (8) using Algorithm 2 under different specifications of κ0. True signal size is κ = 1, indicated by a vertical black line. For each value of κ0 and Cγ , we report the proportion of simulation runs which resulted in false rejections (type I error) and correct rejections (Power). The horizontal red line indicates the 10% threshold for type I error control and the horizontal blue line indicates the 90% threshold for power. 24 5.2.2 Detectability regimes 

We empirically investigate the detectability of the signal strength κ as a function of the sample size 

n and the dimension p. We set κ0 = κ, matching the true signal strength at each value of κ, and fix Cγ = 0 .1. We define detection as reliable for ( n, κ, δ ) when both Type I and Type II error rates remain below δ for a signal of size κ based on n samples. To characterise the detection threshold, we run Algorithm 2 over two parameter grids. In Figure 4, the top row varies n ∈ { 400 , 500 , . . . , 3000 } and κ ∈ { 0.15 , 0.16 , . . . , 0.5} with p = 100 fixed, whereas the bottom row varies p ∈ { 10 , 20 , . . . , 500 } and κ ∈ { 0.2, 0.22 , . . . , 0.7} with n = 500 fixed. The left and right columns correspond to the Laplace and t distributions, respectively. In all panels of Figure 4, the transition between the red (unreliable) and blue (reliable) regions provides an empirical estimate of the detection threshold, namely the minimum signal strength re-quired for reliable detection as a function of n or p. Holding p fixed, Figures 4a and 4b show that this threshold decreases as n increases. In particular, it exhibits a power-law scaling in n, with κ ∝ n−0.45 

for both the Laplace and t-distribution settings. Meanwhile, holding n fixed, Figures 4c and 4d show that the detection threshold increases with p. The empirical relationship is well approximated by 

κ ∝ p0.20 for the Laplace distribution and κ ∝ p0.18 for the t-distribution. These empirical findings are broadly consistent with the rate κ ∝ p0.25 n−0.5 established in Theorems 4 and S14. Although the full theoretical bounds contain additional terms, we conjecture that the parameter ranges considered in Figure 4 lie in the regime n ≳ √p, where the term κ ∝ p0.25 n−0.5 dominates in the Laplace setting. 

5.3 Multivariate online change point detection 

In this subsection, we illustrate the four regimes of detection delay using a slight variant of the proposed multivariate change point detector, Algorithm 4; see Algorithm S4 in Section S5.1 for the full modified algorithm. The main modification concerns the allocation of the false alarm probability. In Algorithm 4, the level α is distributed over all potential tests by a union bound, leading to a per-test level of order t−2(t + 1) −1. This can be overly conservative since tests are only performed at times for which the outlier-control condition u ≤ Ω holds. We therefore allocate the error budget only over executed tests. If tr denotes the r-th time at which testing is performed, then for each potential change location s ≤ tr/2 considered at time tr, we assign a significance level of order αt −1 

> r

r−2. Since there are at most tr such locations at time tr, the total budget at the r-th executed testing time is 

O(αr −2), and hence the overall false alarm probability remains controlled by a union bound. In our simulations, we consider n independent observations {Xi}ni=1 in Rp arriving sequentially, where p = 10. For each i ∈ { 1, . . . , n }, the observation is drawn from the distribution Qi defined in (1), with contamination proportion set to be ε = 0 .01. The outlier distribution Hi is chosen to be 

N (−1p, I ) and we consider the following two choices for the inlier distribution Fi:

• (Laplace( fi, 1/√2)) ⊗p;

• (t(fi, σ = 1 , ν = 4 .1)) ⊗p.The inputs of Algorithm S4 are chosen as follows: the signal size parameter is set to κ0 = 0 .5, the filter strength to Cγ = 0 .1, and the minimum sample size to ht = 1. We set the desired false alarm probability to α = 0 .1. To achieve this level of control, we calibrate the remaining parameters (namely, the detection sensitivity factor Tu, the outlier control threshold Ω and the group number constant Kc) under the no-change setting so that the empirical false alarm rate does not exceed α.To empirically evaluate the performance of Algorithm S4 under a change, we set a single change point at ∆, where the mean fi of the inlier distribution Fi undergoes a jump of magnitude κ:

fi =

(

0 if i ≤ ∆, (κ/ √p)1p if i > ∆. and Ω = 0 .09. The regime transitions are illustrated in Figure 5: Figure 5a shows the detection rate as a function of κ for small jump sizes, while Figure 5b plots the mean detection delay against κ

for larger jump sizes. Analogous plots for the t-distribution are presented in Figure 6, where we set 

n = 7500 and ∆ = 3000, with inputs Tu = (1 − 0.4u)2, Kc = 1, and Ω = 0 .15. The larger values of 

n and ∆ reflect the increased difficulty of robust detection under the t-distribution, which has only a finite fourth moment. Firstly, Figures 5a and 6a show that the calibrated parameters control the false alarm probability at level α ≤ 0.1. As in Regime 1 of the univariate setting, the change point is effectively undetectable by our procedure when κ is small. For example, when κ ≤ 0.05 in Figure 5a or κ ≤ 0.15 in Figure 6a, the empirical probability of raising an alarm during the monitoring period is essentially the same as the false alarm rate under no change. This illustrates the necessity of Assumption 4: effective detection is only possible once the signal strength exceeds a minimum threshold. By contrast, once κ ≥ 0.5, the change is detected with probability at least 0 .9 for both settings. This mirrors the transition between Regimes 1 and 2 observed in the univariate setting. Secondly, within the detectable regime, Figures 5b and 6b show that the mean detection delay initially decreases rapidly as κ increases, before the rate of decrease slows and eventually plateaus. This behaviour is consistent with the three regimes predicted by Theorems 6 and 7, and mirrors Regimes 2–4 in the univariate setting. For moderate κ, the Laplace setting exhibits an intermediate decay that is well described by d ∝ κ−1. For larger κ, the detection delay stabilises at order one, as predicted by our theory in the strong-signal regime.

The black dotted line represents the nominal false alarm rate control ( α= 0 .1).
> (b) Mean detection delay das afunction of
> κ∈[0 .35 ,4.5]. The fitted curves illustrate the three predicted regimes: d∝κ−2for small κ(pink),
> d∝κ−1for intermediate κ(blue), and d∝1 for large κ(orange). The fits are obtained by weighted least squares, with weights equal to the inverse empir-ical standard deviation of the detection delay at each value of κ.

Figure 5: Illustration of the different regimes with inlier distribution (Laplace( fi, 1/√2)) ⊗p and p = 10. Results are based on running Algorithm S4 on 1000 simulated datasets for each value of κ.

# 6 Discussion 

In this work, we studied online mean change point detection under both Huber contamination and heavy-tailed inlier distributions. For univariate data, we characterised the detection delay across different regimes of signal strength, contamination level and tail behaviour. For multivariate data, we developed robust mean testing procedures and used them as subroutines for online change point detection. Several questions remain open. 27 (a) Empirical false alarm rates and detection rates against κ ∈ [0 , 0.6]. For each κ, we record the propor-tion of simulation runs in which the algorithm stopped at or before time ∆ = 3000 (false alarm), between times 3001 and 7500 (detected), or failed to stop by time 7500 (not detected). The black dotted line rep-resents the nominal false alarm rate control ( α = 0 .1).                        

> (b) Mean detection delay das afunction of
> κ∈[0 .45 ,2]. The fitted curves illustrate the three pre-dicted regimes: d∝κ−2.7for small κ(pink) and d∝1for large κ(orange). The fits are obtained by weighted least squares, with weights equal to the inverse empir-ical standard deviation of the detection delay at each value of κ.

Figure 6: Illustration of the different regimes with inlier distribution ( t(fi, 1, 4.1)) ⊗p and p = 10. Results are based on running Algorithm S4 on 1000 simulated datasets for each value of κ.First, our procedures require prior knowledge of the contamination level ε. A conservative up-per bound can be used in practice, but this may lead to unnecessarily large thresholds and hence larger detection delays. One possible direction is to adapt results on robust mean estimation with unknown contamination level without sacrificing the order of the estimation error (e.g. Jain et al., 2022). For example, in the univariate setting, one could run the median and RUME procedures over a geometric grid of candidate contamination levels, and then select a mean estimator by intersecting the corresponding confidence sets. This approach, however, is currently better suited to estimation than testing. Extending it to robust testing and subsequently to multivariate change point detection is an interesting direction for future work. Second, the multivariate rates obtained in the moderate- and large-signal regimes may not be sharp. This is largely a consequence of relying on the matrix Bern-stein inequality, which may not have fully exploited the tail structure of sub-Weibull or finite-moment inliers. Sharper bounds would likely require a more refined understanding of the spectral properties of empirical covariance matrices under heavy-tailed assumptions. Third, our multivariate theory assumes coordinate-wise independence. This assumption allows us to exploit existing concentration properties of univariate sub-Weibull random variables. Removing it would require new concentration tools for dependent heavy-tailed random vectors, especially under contamination. Developing such tools would broaden the applicability of the proposed methods. Finally, while the present paper focuses primar-ily on statistical optimality and minimax detection delay, it would be useful to design robust online procedures with lower update and storage costs.

The proofs of all theoretical results are presented in the Appendices. A summary of proof dependencies is presented in Section S1. Section S2 contains proofs of the univariate change point results, namely Theorems 1–3. Section S3 contains technical details of the subroutines of Algorithm 2, as well as proofs of the multivariate testing results, namely Propositions 4–5. Section S4 collects the proofs of Theorems 6–7 for the multivariate change point problem. The full pseudocode for the practical multivariate change point algorithm and supplementary plots for Section 5.2 can be found in Section S5. Section S6 contains auxiliary results.

S2.1 Proof of Theorem 1 

The lower bounds in the five cases can be shown via the following propositions. 

Regime 1a 

Regime 1 characterises the information-theoretic lower bounds of the problem. It addresses the sce-narios where Assumptions 2 or 3 are violated. To illustrate Regime 1a, Theorem S1 shows that when contamination level is large relative to the signal strength, there exist inlier and contamination distributions such that any change point estimator is inconsistent. 

Proposition S1. Suppose {Xi}i∈N satisfies Assumption 1, where ε > 0 and 

Qi =

(

(1 − ε)δ(0) + εδ (κ/ε ) = qa, if i ≤ ∆,

(1 − ε)qa + εq a, if i > ∆,where κ ≥ 0. Denote inlier distributions as F1 = δ(0) and F∆+1 = qa.(a) Assume that κ ≤ 2−1/v ϕε 1−1/v for some ϕ > 0 and v ∈ N. Then Fi ∈ P v,ϕ , which implies that Q ∈ Θ(∆ , κ, Pv,ϕ ).(b) Assume that κ/M ≤ ε{log[1 /(2 ε)] }1/θ for some M > 0, θ > 0 and ε ≤ Cθ, where Cθ > 0 is a constant depending on θ. Then Fi ∈ G θ,M , which implies that Q ∈ Θ(∆ , κ, Gθ,M ).With this choice of Q, we have 

inf  

> ˆt∈T (α)

The idea of the proof is that when signal-to-noise ratio is very low, contamination distributions for the pre-change and post-change distributions can be constructed such that Qi are identical for all 

i ∈ N. Thus, we are unable to tell when a change in mean has occurred. 

Proof. (a) We first note that the difference in means before and after the change point satisfies

Then, we show that F1 and F∆+1 have a finite absolute vth central moment upper bounded by ϕv. It is straightforward to show that EX∼F1 [|X − E[X]|v] = 0. For F∆+1 , we have 

EX∼F∆+1 [|X − E[X]|v] = (1 − ε)|0 − κ|v + ε|κ/ε − κ|v

≤ κv(1 + ε1−v) ≤ 2κvε1−v ≤ ϕv,

38 Regime 1b 

At the same time, Theorem S2 establishes the impossibility of detection for the regime where κ ≲

∆−1/2. The proof proceeds by restricting attention to Gaussian inliers without contamination ( ε =0), and showing that any procedure satisfying the false-alarm constraint can have arbitrarily large detection delay with non-zero probability. To be precise, we consider the family of normal distributions with fixed variance σ2, and denote the class of distributions by 

Nσ = {N (μ, σ 2) : μ ∈ R}.

Since Nσ ⊂ G θ,M whenever M ≥ C1σ, and Nσ ⊂ P v,ϕ whenever ϕ ≥ C2σ, for large enough constants constants C1, C 2 > 0, the same lower bound extends to these larger distribution classes. We define the following notation for the proof. Since any measure P ∈ Θ(∆ , κ, Nσ) is uniquely determined by its mean sequence f ∈ S (∆ , κ ), we denote the probability measure associated with a specific sequence f as Pf . This identification similarly holds for the null space, where each P0 ∈ Θ0(Nσ)is uniquely associated with a constant mean sequence f ∈ S 0.

Regime 2 

For Regime 2, Yu et al. (2023) established a minimax lower bound for the normal family in the absence of contamination; this result is recalled in Theorem S3. By the same inclusion argument as in Regime 1b, this lower bound extends to the larger distribution classes Gθ,M and Pv,ϕ , provided that M and ϕ

are sufficiently large.

Regime 3 

We now derive a minimax lower bound on the expected detection delay over the inlier distributions belonging to the class of distributions with finite v-th moment, Pv,ϕ . Rather than considering the whole class of distributions, it is sufficient to consider the case where the inlier distributions belong to the symmetric type II Pareto family and there is no contamination ( ε = 0). To be specific, let Ps = {Par( μ, s ) : μ ∈ R} denote the set of symmetric type II Pareto distributions with shape parameter s > 2, where the density is given by 

p(x; μ, s ) = s − 12 (1 + |x − μ|)−s, x ∈ R.

The proof adapts arguments used in Theorem 2 of Lai (1998).

Regime 4 

Finally, we discuss Regime 4, the case where the signal is very large. To show a minimax lower bound on the the expected detection delay, the idea here is to construct a distribution on two points {0, κ }

and derive a lower bound on the expected detection delay for this model.

S3.1 Notation and preliminaries 

In this subsection, we introduce the notation and probabilistic preliminaries needed for the design and analysis of our robust mean testing procedure, RobustMeanTest (Algorithm 2). 

Good and bad sets 

Let Y = {Y1, . . . , Y n} be the sample of n random variables generated as follows. Let di ∼ Bern( ε)denote the contamination label, so that ( Yi | di = 0) ∼ F and ( Yi | di = 1) ∼ Hi. Fix a truncation radius R > 0, to be specified later. We define the good and bad index sets as 

G = {i ∈ [n] : ∥Yi − μ∥2 ≤ R, d i = 0 }, B = [ n] \ G.

Throughout this section, Y, G, and B will always refer to these sets. Define 

q = 1 − P(∥Y1 − μ∥2 ≤ R, d 1 = 0) , γ = PY ∼F (∥Y − μ∥2 > R ).

Then 

q = 1 − PY1∼F (∥Y1 − μ∥2 ≤ R) P(d1 = 0) = 1 − (1 − γ)(1 − ε) ≤ ε + γ. 

Since |B| ∼ Binom( n, q ), Bernstein’s inequality (Theorem 2.9.5, Vershynin, 2026) implies that, with probability at least 1 − δ,

|B| ≤ un, where u = q +

r 2q log(1 /δ )

n + 2 log(1 /δ )3n . (S36) Denote event A = {|B| ≤ un }. For our proofs below, we will assume our variables ε, p, n, δ satisfy 

u ≤ 0.08.

Weights and linear-algebraic quantities 

Our algorithm will assign weights to each point, that we will monotonically decrease over time. For any n, let Γ n denote the set of valid weights: Γn = {w ∈ Rn : wi ∈ [0 , 1] for all i = 1 , . . . , n }.

Recall that for any set M ⊆ Y , 1M ∈ Γn denotes the indicator vector for M, and 1 = 1S .Let N be some value to be specified later. Given a set of points Y = {Y1, . . . , Y n}, we associate it weight vectors w(t) ∈ Γn, for i = 1 , . . . , n and t = 1 , . . . , N where initially we set w(1) = 1. For any such weight vector w, we let Sum( w, Y) = X

> i∈Y

When the context is clear, we will drop the Y from the notation for simplicity. For any set M, and for any set of weights w on Y, we let wM denote the set of weights restricted to the indices in M ∩ Y .We also let Gram( w, Y) be the n × n matrix given by Gram( w, Y)ij = √wiwj ⟨Yi, Y j ⟩.

Note that by design, the nontrivial eigenvalues of Gram( w, Y) and M (w, Y) are identical. We define the following important set: Λn = {w ∈ Γn : ∥1G − wG∥1 ≤ 5 ∥1B − wB∥1}, (S40) that is, Λ n is the set of weights where we have removed at most five times as much weight from the good samples as we have removed from the the bad samples. 

Downweighting scheme

The downweighting procedure defined in Proposition S7 ensures that the influence of contaminated points is systematically diminished. The key feature of this procedure is that the weights before and after downweighting remain in the set Λ n, which simplifies the analysis of the algorithm. 54 Concentration of second moment matrix 

Our filtering algorithm relies on inspecting the operator norm of the second moment matrix. Thus, we need the following concentration inequalities.

If an ε-net argument is used, the slower tail decay of sub-Weibull distributions incurs a p1/θ

Remark S3. The condition Cγ κ20/2 ≥ κ2 can be relaxed as follows. By pairing data points and taking pairwise differences, e.g. Zj = Y2j − Y2j−1 for j ∈ { 1, . . . , ⌊n/ 2⌋} , we create a dataset of size ⌊n/ 2⌋

with mean 0. When we apply the same filtering approach to this dataset, the nκ 20 term in (S43) , that originally arose from the rank-1 contribution O(n)μμ ⊤ in the upper bound of ∥M (G) − nI ∥op , will not appear in the empirical second moment matrix of the pairwise-difference dataset. Hence, we can remove the condition Cγ κ20/10 ≥ κ2 using this approach, though the minimum signal strength required for type I and II error control will remain unchanged.

The condition (S46) plays a similar role as (9), but is adapted to the setting where the inlier distribution possesses only finite v-th moments. In particular, u is defined as an upper bound on the fraction of outliers (i.e. contaminated samples and inlier samples far from the mean) within the dataset, and the bound holds with probability at least 1 − δ/ 4. Comparing with Theorem 4, we observe two key differences. Firstly, the requirements on κ0 and 

κ have a polynomial dependence on δ in Theorem S14, while that in Theorem 4 have a logarithmic dependence on δ. Having polynomial dependence is not an issue if we consider δ as fixed and large, but it is not ideal when it is used as a subroutine to the change point problem. We address this issue in Section 3.2. Secondly, we now have a new regime for n ≤ p, where we observe that the minimum signal strength is of order p and does not improve as n increases. This could be an artefact of the proof and detailed reasoning is provided in Remark S4. The proof of Theorem S14 will follow closely to the proof of Theorem 4. We will consider the same four conditions that hold with probability 1 − δ. Only the choice of constants β1 and β2 differ.

Remark S4. Theorem S14 shows that, for the class Ppv,ϕ , the minimum values of κ and κ0 required for type I and II error control do not improve with n in the regime n ≤ p. This feature can be traced to its proof. The analysis of Algorithm 2 requires controlling the number of inliers which lie outside a 

ℓ2-ball centred at the mean with radius R, which happens with probability γ with an upper bound given in (S38) . Hence the issue is tied to the use of Markov’s inequality and the choice of R in (S37) . When 

n > p , one can choose R2 − p ≍ √n so that the bound is of order (p/n )v/ 4. However, when n ≤ p,this choice of R is not useful since (p/n )v/ 4 ≥ O(1) . To keep the proportion of truncated inliers under control, we instead take R2 − p ≍ √p, which yields a constant-order bound on γ. Consequently, in the regime n ≤ p, the outlier proportion u given in (S39) is of constant order, so the term u2p log(1 /u )

is of order p. This term is then dominant in (S47) and (S48) . The same reasoning also applies to Theorem 5, since the proof relies on Algorithm 2 as a subroutine within Algorithm 3.

69 S3.10 Proof of Lemma S11 

Proof. The runtime per iteration is clearly dominated by the time it takes to find the top singular vector of the centred gram matrix, which can be done in time O(pn 2). We will show that for all t = 1 , . . . , N , we have that w(t) ∈ Λn. First, we demonstrate how this proves the overall lemma. First, note that after each iteration, some new wi (with the maximum τi)becomes 0, so after 6 un iterations, we have removed at least 6 un mass from w. By definition of Λ n in (S40), this means we have removed at least un mass from the bad coordinates wB, at which point no further updates can maintain the invariant that w(i) ∈ Λn.Next, we observe that if w(N ) ∈ Λn, then since we terminated, we must have that 

Gram( w(N )) − D(w(N )) ≤ 5Rf .

But then, for all M with |M| ≤ un , let 1M ∈ Rn be the indicator vector on the set M. Then, we have that
