# S4 Technical details and proofs of results in Section 4 

S4.1 Properties of the pairwise difference dataset 

We first briefly recall the data generation process in online change point detection. For each i ∈ [n], 

Xi is independently generated from Qi, where Qi is defined in (1) by 

Qi = (1 − εi)Fi + εiHi, i ∈ { 1, . . . , n },

where εi ≤ ε for all i. Suppose we observe {X1, . . . , X t}, where t ∈ N. For 1 ≤ s ≤ ⌊ t/ 2⌋, define the pairwise difference 

Ys,t = Xt−s+1 − Xs

√2σ .

Theorem S17 shows that {Ys,t }⌊t/ 2⌋ 

> s=1

is equal in distribution to a Huber contamination model. In particular, in the pre-change regime ( t ≤ ∆), the inlier distribution will have zero mean, while in the post-change regime ( t > ∆ ≥ s), the inlier distribution will share a common mean with norm 

κ/ √2̸ = 0, allowing us to apply our robust mean testing procedure. 

Proposition S17 (Huber structure of pairwise differences) . Fix t ∈ N. For each s ∈ { 1, . . . , ⌊t/ 2⌋} ,the random variable Ys,t follows a dynamic Huber 2ε-contamination model 

Ys,t ∼ Qs,t = (1 − 2ε)Ds,t + 2 εH s,t ,

where Ds,t is an inlier distribution with the following properties: (a) EY ∼Ds,t [Y ] = ( ft−s+1 − fs)/(√2σ). In particular, 

EY ∼Ds,t [Y ] 2 =

(

0 if t ≤ ∆,

κ/ √2 if t > ∆ ≥ s.(b) Var Y ∼Ds,t [Y ] = Ip, thus Ds,t ∈ P p 

> 2,1

for all s ∈ { 1, . . . , ⌊t/ 2⌋} .

77 (c) Assuming further that Fi ∈ G pθ,M for all i ∈ N, then Ds,t ∈ G pθ, √2M/σ for all s ∈ { 1, . . . , ⌊t/ 2⌋} .Proof. Let Bi denote the event that Xi is not contaminated, i.e. Xi is drawn from the inlier distribution 

Fi. Then P(Bi) = 1 − εi, and for any fixed s, t we have 

P(Bt−s+1 ∩ Bs) = (1 − εt−s+1 )(1 − εs) ≥ (1 − ε)2 ≥ 1 − 2ε, 

since εi ≤ ε for all i. Conditional on the event Bt−s+1 ∩ Bs, the random variable Ys,t has the same distribution as (Zt−s+1 − Zs)/(√2σ),

where Zt−s+1 ∼ Ft−s+1 and Zs ∼ Fs are independent. Direct evaluation of the mean and variance gives the result.

S6.1 Basic properties of sub-Weibull random variables 

Definition S1 (Orlicz norms) . Let f : [0 , ∞) → [0 , ∞) be a non-decreasing function with f (0) = 0 .The f-Orlicz norm of a real-valued random variable X is 

∥X∥f = inf {t > 0 : E f (|X|/t ) ≤ 1}.

Definition S2 (Sub-Weibull random variables) . A random variable X is sub-Weibull with parameter 

θ > 0, denoted sub-Weibull (θ), if 

∥X∥ψθ < ∞,

with the function ψθ defined by ψθ(x) = exp( xθ) − 1 for x ≥ 0.

Lemma S20 (Vladimirova et al. 2020, Theorem 2.1) . Let X be a sub-Weibull (θ) random variable with 

θ > 0 and ∥X∥ψθ = M < ∞. Then, we have the following properties. (a) For any x ≥ 0, it holds that 

P(|X| ≥ x) ≤ 2 exp {− (x/M )θ}.

(b) There exists an absolute constant Kθ > 0 such that 

(E |X|k)1/k ≤ KθM k 1/θ , ∀k ≥ 1.

We now provide two tail bound results from literature for sums and quadratic forms of independent sub-Weibull random variables respectively. Theorem S22 below can be viewed as an extension of the Hanson–Wright inequality (Hanson and Wright, 1971). 

Lemma S21 (Hao et al. (2019), Theorem 3.1) . Suppose {Xi}ni=1 are independent sub-Weibull random variables with ∥Xi∥ψθ ≤ M . Then there exists an absolute constant Cθ only depending on θ such that for any a ∈ Rn and 0 < δ < e −2,

S6.2 Properties of univariate robust mean estimators 

A key part in our proposed univariate change point procedure is the use of robust estimators, namely the Optimal Robust Univariate Mean Estimator (Prasad et al., 2020) and the median. We will outline their statistical properties in the following section, which are crucial in proving results on our change point procedure in Section 2. 

Medians

Remark S6. By a very similar argument, if there exists c ∈ R such that P(Xi < c ) ≤ 1/4 for all i,then 

P(median( X1: n) < c ) ≤ exp( −n/ 8) .

Optimal Robust Univariate Mean Estimation 

The Optimal Robust Univariate Mean Estimator (Prasad et al., 2020) enables robust estimation of the inlier mean under the dynamic Huber contamination model (1), even when F is heavy-tailed. A slightly-modified version of their approach is described in Algorithm S5. Algorithm S5 is similar to that of trimmed means (Lugosi and Mendelson, 2021). Both approaches split the dataset into two subsamples of equal size. One subsample is used to learn a truncation interval, and the other is used to compute the resulting truncated empirical mean. The difference lies in how they pick the truncation intervals. While trimmed means estimates the q-th sample quantile and (1 − q)-th sample quantile for some q ∈ (0 , 1), Algorithm S5 picks a shortest interval ˆI containing 1 − 2q fraction of observations. 1

Algorithm S5 Robust Univariate Mean Estimation (RUME) 

INPUT: Dataset {zi}2ni=1 , Corruption Level ε, Confidence Level δ

Split the data into two subsets: Z1 = {zi}ni=1 and Z2 = {zi}2ni=n+1 .Let ε′ = max

Lemma 3 of Prasad et al. (2020) shows that RUME is information theoretically optimal in the case where the inlier distribution F has finite variance σ2. The estimation error of RUME is of the order 

O(σ√ε′). Li and Yu (2021) has generalised the proof to study the dynamic Huber contamination model setting stated in Definition 1 under the finite variance assumption of F . Here, we further generalise the proof for F ∈ G θ,M and F ∈ P v,ϕ .

Theorem S29 shows that there are two regimes for the RUME estimation error, depending on whether the contamination term ([ ε′]1−1/v in finite moment setting or ε′ log 1+1 /θ   1

> ε′

 in sub-Weibull setting) or the sampling term plog(1 /δ )/h dominates. The existence of these two regimes imply the optimality for mean estimation under heavy-tailed data and Huber contamination. In the absence of contamination ( ε = 0), we recover the sub-Gaussian rate of estimation O(plog(1 /δ )/h ) for both classes of inlier distributions. When contamination level is large, the RUME estimation error matches the information theoretic lower bound for mean estimation under Huber contamination when F0 ∈ P v,ϕ 

(Steinhardt et al., 2018).

S6.4 Concentration inequalities for the multivariate setting 

Throughout this subsection, we work under the following setup, which is also adopted in the proofs in Section S3. Let X1, . . . , X n be independent random variables in Rp drawn from the Huber contam-ination model 

Xi ∼ Di := (1 − ε)F + εH i, i = 1 , . . . , n, 

where F ∈ D is the inlier distribution and H1, . . . , H n are arbitrary contamination distributions. Equivalently, we may represent the Huber contamination model by introducing independent contam-ination indicators 

di i.i.d.

∼ Bern( ε), i = 1 , . . . , n. 

Then di = 0 indicates that the observation is drawn from the inlier distribution F , while di = 1 indicates that it is drawn from the contamination distribution Hi. Thus, (Xi | di = 0) ∼ F, (Xi | di = 1) ∼ Hi.

Recall that we define the set of inliers that are close to the mean as 

G = {i ∈ [n] : di = 0 , ∥Xi − μ∥2 ≤ R}.

We therefore introduce the following random variables to aid the exposition. For each i ∈ [n], let 

ζi = 1 {di = 0 , ∥Xi − μ∥2 ≤ R} .

We also define

100 Lemma S43 (General sample covariance matrix shift) . Let v ≥ 4. Suppose that Z ∈ Rp is sampled from a distribution F ∈ P pv,ϕ with mean 0 and variance I. Then, for any event E which occurs with probability at least 1 − δ ≥ 12 ,

I − E[ZZ ⊤|E] op ≤ O(δ1−2/v ).

In particular, if v = 4 ,
